g_{\theta}(r)=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}v_{D}^{-1}\Psi_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta),\quad G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}g_{\theta}(r),\quad G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta)=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{g_{\theta}(r)|D}\right],

and
\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}
uniquely solves
G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})=0
.
Note that
\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}
uniquely solves
G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})=0
.

\dot{G}_{{\hat{\eta}}}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}v_{D}^{-1}\dot{\Psi}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}});

\dot{G}_{{\bar{\eta}}}=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}v_{D}^{-1}\dot{\Psi}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\Bigm|D}\right];

\hat{V}_{G}=\left(\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\right)\left(\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\right)^{T};

\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta)=\left(\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})\psi^{T}_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})\right);

\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta)=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\theta,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})\psi^{T}_{\theta,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})\right);

\mathcal{V}_{\eta}(\theta)=P\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{\eta}(\theta)
.
Note that

\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\psi_{\theta,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})\psi^{T}_{\theta,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})\right)=\sqrt{n}\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{\theta,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}+o_{P}(1)

from
eq.

E.11
(the equality holds without
o_{P}(1)
if
K>1
).

\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}}^{2}=V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}}^{-1}\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}})\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}})^{T};

v_{D}^{2}=\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\bigm|D\right];

\hat{v}_{D}^{2}=\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-2}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\hat{V}_{G}\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})^{T};

\zeta_{D}^{2}=\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[2^{-1}\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-3}(h(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})-\tau)V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}})\left(\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{T}\Bigm|D\right];

\hat{a}=(\hat{a}_{1}\;\cdots\;\hat{a}_{d})=\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1};

\hat{v}_{(i,j)}
are the entries of
\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})
,

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\frac{1}{b}\sum_{\ell\in s}\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},i}(W_{\ell})\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},j}(W_{\ell})-\hat{v}_{(i,j)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\frac{1}{b}\sum_{\ell\in s}\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},i^{\prime}}(W_{\ell})\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},j^{\prime}}(W_{\ell})-\hat{v}_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\right);

\hat{\zeta}_{D}^{2}=2^{-2}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-6}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)^{2}V_{M,K}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{d}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{d}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{i^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})};

\displaystyle\rho_{v,\zeta}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Cov}_{P}\Biggl[\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}),

\displaystyle 2^{-1}\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-3}(h(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})-\tau)V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}})\left(\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{T}\Biggm|D\Biggr];

\hat{d}_{i,(j,\ell)}=\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},i}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\hat{\Psi}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1},i}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\right)\left(\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i^{\prime}\in s}\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},j}(W_{i^{\prime}})\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},\ell}(W_{i^{\prime}})-\hat{v}_{(j,\ell)}\right);

\hat{\rho}_{v,\zeta}=2^{-1}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-4}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)V_{M,K}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{\ell}\hat{d}_{i,(j,\ell)};

\sigma^{2}_{D}=2(v_{D}^{2}+\zeta_{D}^{2}+2\rho_{v,\zeta});

(B.17)

\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{D}=2(\hat{v}_{D}^{2}+\hat{\zeta}_{D}^{2}+2\hat{\rho}_{v,\zeta}).

Assumption B.4
.

The following conditions hold:

(i)

For any
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
and
\varepsilon>0
,

P\left(\sup_{\left\lVert\theta-\theta^{\prime}\right\rVert<\delta_{n}}\left\lVert\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}}-G_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)(\theta)-\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}}-G_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)(\theta^{\prime})\right\rVert>\varepsilon\Biggm|D\right)\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
;

(ii)

For
(i,j)\in\left[d\right]^{2}
,
E.1
holds with
T=\Theta^{\prime}
and

\mathcal{F}_{\eta}=\left\{\psi_{\theta,\eta,i}\psi_{\theta,\eta,j}:\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}\right\},

where
\psi_{\theta,\eta,i}
is the
i
-th coordinate of
\psi_{\theta,\eta}
;

(iii)

There exists an estimator
\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}}
such that

\left\lVert\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right\rVert\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
;

(iv)

For some
\underline{v}>0
,

\sigma^{2}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}=\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})V_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{T}\geq\underline{v};

(v)

M^{-1}n\sigma^{2}_{D}=O_{P}(1)
.

(vi)

Either

v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\xrightarrow{P}c_{1}\neq 1

or

2\frac{\rho_{v,\zeta}}{\zeta_{D}v_{D}}\xrightarrow{P}c_{2}\neq-1.

∎

B.4(i)
is a Donsker condition on
\left\{v_{D}^{-1}\Psi_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}:s\subseteq\left[n\right]\right\}
conditional on the data.
It is similar to
B.1
, and can typically be verified using arguments similar to the ones used to verify
E.1(vi)
.
It holds, for example, if
\Theta^{\prime}
is bounded and
\psi_{\theta,\eta}
is Lipschitz in
\theta
with a Lipschitz constant that does not depend on
\eta
or
w
(see, e.g., Example 19.7 in
50
).

B.4(ii)
is a Donsker condition similar to
B.1(i)
, but in terms of the product
\psi_{\theta,\eta,i}\psi_{\theta,\eta,j}
instead of
\psi_{\theta,\eta,i}
.
It is used to derive asymptotic normality of the standard errors
\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}},\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}
.
If
\psi_{\theta,\eta,i}(w)\leq\bar{C}
for some
\bar{C}<\infty
, that is, if the functions
\psi_{\theta,\eta,i}
are uniformly bounded, then
B.4(ii)
is implied by
B.1(i)
(see, e.g., Example 2.10.10 in
49
).

B.4(iii)
assumes the existence of a consistent estimator of
\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
.
If
\psi_{\theta,\eta}(w)
is differentiable in
\theta
, the plug-in estimator defined in
eq.

B.10
satisfies this assumption under a uniform integrability condition on this derivative.
Otherwise, consistent estimators can typically be constructed on a case-by-case basis
(
25
)
.

B.4(iv)
requires the asymptotic variance of
h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}})
to be lower bounded.

B.4(v)
establishes the asymptotic regime.
Finally,
B.4(vi)
restricts a corner case where the variance of the t-statistic
\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)
is zero because of perfect negative correlation between
\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}
and
h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})
.
Note that the quantities
\rho_{v,\zeta},\zeta_{D},v_{D}
can all be consistently estimated with
\hat{\rho}_{v,\zeta},\hat{\zeta}_{D},\hat{v}_{D}
defined previously.

Before proving
5.1
, I establish some key intermediary results.

Lemma B.3
.

Let the conditions of
5.1
hold.
Then,

\sigma_{D}^{-1}v_{D}=O_{P}(1),\quad\sigma_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}=O_{P}(1).

Proof.

I show
\sigma_{D}^{-1}v_{D}=O_{P}(1)
and the second result follows analogously.

\sigma^{2}_{D}=2(v_{D}^{2}+\zeta_{D}^{2}+2\rho_{v,\zeta}),

\sigma^{-2}_{D}v_{D}^{2}=2^{-1}(1+v_{D}^{-2}\zeta_{D}^{2}+2v_{D}^{-2}\rho_{v,\zeta})^{-1},

\displaystyle v_{D}^{-2}\zeta_{D}^{2}+2v_{D}^{-2}\rho_{v,\zeta}

\displaystyle=v_{D}^{-2}\zeta_{D}^{2}+2v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\frac{\rho_{v,\zeta}}{\zeta_{D}v_{D}}

\displaystyle=v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\left(v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}+2\frac{\rho_{v,\zeta}}{\zeta_{D}v_{D}}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}O_{P}(1)\text{, if }v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}=O_{P}(1),\\
o_{P}(1)\text{, if }v_{D}\zeta_{D}^{-1}=o_{P}(1),\end{cases}

since
\left|\frac{\rho_{v,\zeta}}{\zeta_{D}v_{D}}\right|\leq 1
.
Note that

v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\left(v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}+2\frac{\rho_{v,\zeta}}{\zeta_{D}v_{D}}\right)\xrightarrow{P}-1\iff v_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\xrightarrow{P}1\land 2\frac{\rho_{v,\zeta}}{\zeta_{D}v_{D}}\xrightarrow{P}-1,

which is ruled out by
B.4(vi)
.
∎

Theorem B.2
.

Let
B.4
hold.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

P\left(\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)-\left(-v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)\right\rVert>\varepsilon\Bigm|D\right)\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
, and hence

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)-\left(-v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)\right\rVert>\varepsilon\right)\to 0.

Moreover,

v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})=O_{P}(1).

∎

Proof of
B.2
.

For a random variable
X_{M}
and a deterministic (conditional on
D
) sequence
a_{M}(D)
, I use
X_{M}=o_{P|D}(a_{M}(D))
to denote

P\left(\left\lVert\frac{X_{M}}{a_{M}(D)}\right\rVert>\varepsilon\Bigm|D\right)\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
for any
\varepsilon>0
,
and analogously define
O_{P|D}(a_{M}(D))
similar to the
O_{P}
notation.

By differentiability of
G
,

\displaystyle v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})-G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)

\displaystyle=v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\dot{G}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)+o_{P|D}\left(\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)\right\rVert\right).

(B.18)

Further,

\displaystyle\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})-G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})-G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})\right)

(B.19)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)+o_{P|D}(1)

(B.20)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})+o_{P|D}(1)

(B.21)

\displaystyle=O_{P|D}(1).

(B.22)

eq.

B.19
uses the definitions
G_{{\bar{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})=G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})=0
, and
eq.

B.20
uses
B.4(i)
.

eq.

B.22
follows from the Lindeberg CLT.

Combining
eqs.

B.18
and
B.21
gives

\displaystyle v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})+o_{P|D}\left(\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)\right\rVert\right)+o_{P|D}\left(\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right\rVert\right)

\displaystyle=-v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})+o_{P|D}\left(1\right),

since

v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\dot{\Psi}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\Bigm|D}\right]^{-1}=\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})+o_{P}(1)=O_{P}(1)

by
B.1(v)
, and an argument similar to
eq.

B.9
, exploring
eq.

B.22
, gives

\sqrt{M}\left\lVert\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right\rVert=O_{P|D}(1).

The second result follows since, for any events
A
and
B
,

P(A|B)=o_{P}(1)\implies\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P(A)=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{P(A|B)}\right]\to 0.

Finally,

\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right\rVert\left\lVert\sqrt{M}G_{{\hat{\eta}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right\rVert=O_{P}(1)O_{P|D}(1).

∎

Proof of
5.1
.

The proof is divided into three main steps.
First, I show that

v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}))=-\dot{h}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})v_{D}^{-1}\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)+o_{P}\left(1\right).

(B.23)

Second, I show that

\displaystyle\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}\right)

(B.24)

\displaystyle=(2\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{-1}V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)\left(\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{T}+o_{P}(1).

Finally, I combine the previous steps to reach the result.

Step one.

\displaystyle v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}))

\displaystyle=v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}))-v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}))+v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}))

\displaystyle=v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}))+o_{P}(1),

since

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\right)

\displaystyle=\dot{h}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})+o_{P}\left(\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}\right\rVert\right)

\displaystyle=\dot{h}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})-\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})\right)+o_{P}(1),

where
o_{P}\left(\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}\right\rVert\right)=o_{P}(1)
by
3.1
, the second equality holds from
3.1
, and the last equality from
E.1
, using the fact that
\left\lVert\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right\rVert=o_{P}(1)
.
Note that
v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}/\sqrt{n}=O_{P}(1)
from
Lemma

B.3
.

By differentiability of
h
,

v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)=v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})+o_{P}\left(1\right),

since
v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left\lVert\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right\rVert=O_{P}(1)
from
B.2
.
This implies

v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)=\dot{h}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})+o_{P}\left(1\right).

B.2
gives

\displaystyle v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)-v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}-\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}v_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)+o_{P}(1).

eq.

B.23
follows from combining the two previous displays.

Step two.

\displaystyle\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2})-\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})+\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})+o_{P}(1),

since

\displaystyle\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2})-\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\sqrt{n}}\zeta_{D}^{-1}\right)\left(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2})-\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\left(\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2})-\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})\right),

and

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2})-\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\right)\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})^{T}

\displaystyle\quad-V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})^{T}

\displaystyle=V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})^{T}

\displaystyle\quad-V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})^{T}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=o_{P}(1),

where the second equality follows from
B.4(ii)
and
E.1
, and the last equality uses
\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\mathcal{V}}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)=O_{P}(1)
.

Finally,

\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})=\frac{\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})}{\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}+\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}=(2\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{-1}\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})+o_{P}(1),

and

\displaystyle\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{2}-\sigma_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle=V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)\left(\widehat{\dot{\Psi}}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})^{T}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)\left(\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{T}+o_{P}(1),

using the fact that
\zeta_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)=O_{P}(1)
.

Step three.

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\sigma}_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(\frac{h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\sigma}_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\frac{h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\hat{\sigma}_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\frac{h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\frac{h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})}{\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}}-\sigma_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}(\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}-\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\frac{h(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})-\tau}{\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{2}}+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\dot{G}^{-1}_{{\bar{\eta}}}\sigma_{D}^{-1}\sqrt{M}\left(G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})-G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\right)+o_{P}\left(\sigma_{D}^{-1}v_{D}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-2^{-1}\sigma_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-3}(h(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})-\tau)V_{M,K}\dot{h}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}})\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{M}}{\sigma_{D}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})\right)\left(\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}^{-1}\right)^{T}\dot{h}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})^{T}

\displaystyle\quad+o_{P}\left(\sigma_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\right)+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathcal{N}(0,1),

conditional on
D
with probability approaching one, by Lindeberg’s CLT, by definition of
\sigma_{D}
, and since
G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}})\perp G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}),\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})
and
\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})\perp G_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\theta_{{\bar{\eta}}}),\mathcal{V}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})
conditional on
D
.
Note that
o_{P}\left(\sigma_{D}^{-1}v_{D}\right),o_{P}\left(\sigma_{D}^{-1}\zeta_{D}\right)=o_{P}(1)
by
Lemma

B.3
.
∎

Proof of
5.2
.

For
(p_{j},\hat{\delta}(\beta))=(p_{j}^{+},\hat{\delta}^{+}(\beta))
,

\displaystyle P\left(p_{2}^{+}>p_{1}^{+}+\hat{\delta}^{+}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\Phi\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)>\Phi\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right)+\hat{\delta}^{+}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\Phi\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)>\Phi\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\right)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}>-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)<\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=\beta+o_{P}(1),

where the last equality follows from
5.1
.

For
(p_{j},\hat{\delta}(\beta))=(p_{j}^{-},\hat{\delta}^{-}(\beta))
,

\displaystyle P\left(p_{2}^{-}>p_{1}^{-}+\hat{\delta}^{-}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\Phi\left(-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)>\Phi\left(-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right)+\hat{\delta}^{-}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\Phi\left(-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)>\Phi\left(-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\right)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}>-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)>-\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=1-\Phi\left(-\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\right)+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\beta+o_{P}(1).

For
(p_{j},\hat{\delta}(\beta))=(p_{j}^{\pm},\hat{\delta}^{\pm}(\beta))
,

\displaystyle P\left(p_{2}^{\pm}>p_{1}^{\pm}+\hat{\delta}^{\pm}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(2\Phi\left(-\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right|\right)>2\Phi\left(-\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right|\right)+\hat{\delta}^{\pm}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(2\Phi\left(-\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right|\right)>2\Phi\left(-\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right|-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta/2)\right)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right|-\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right|>-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta/2)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right|>-\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta/2)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=2P\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}<\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\Phi^{-1}(\beta/2)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=2P\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}\right)<\Phi^{-1}(\beta/2)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=2\Phi\left(\Phi^{-1}(\beta/2)\right)+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=\beta+o_{P}(1).

∎

Proof of
5.3
.

The first result follows since, from the proof of
5.1
,

\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}\right)=O_{P}(1).

For
(p_{j},\hat{\delta}(\beta))=(p_{j}^{+},\hat{\delta}^{+}(\beta))
, from the proof of
5.2
,

\displaystyle P\left(p_{2}^{+}>p_{1}^{+}+\hat{\delta}^{+}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}<\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\right)\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\Biggm|D\right),

which converges to zero since

\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{1}}}-\frac{\sqrt{n}(h(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}})-\tau)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{2}}}=O_{P}(1)

from
5.1
, and

\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\hat{\sigma}_{D}}{\sqrt{M}}\right)\Phi^{-1}(\beta)\xrightarrow{P}-\infty

since
\Phi^{-1}(\beta)<0
.
Analogous results follow for
(p_{j}^{-},\hat{\delta}^{-}(\beta))
and
(p_{j}^{\pm},\hat{\delta}^{\pm}(\beta))
.
∎

B.4.2
Fraction Per Tercile as a Z-Estimator

For a given split
\mathsf{s}
, the vector

\left(\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}Y_{i}\mathbb{I}\left(\hat{t}_{j-1,\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}<{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(X_{i})\leq\hat{t}_{j,\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)}{\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\mathbb{I}\left(\hat{t}_{j-1,\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}<{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(X_{i})\leq\hat{t}_{j,\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)}\right)_{j=1}^{3},(\hat{t}_{j,\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})_{j=1}^{2}\right)^{T}

is a Z-estimator with the moment functions

\psi_{(\theta,t),\eta}(y,x)=\begin{pmatrix}y\mathbb{I}\left(t_{0}<\eta(x)\leq t_{1}\right)-\theta_{1}\mathbb{I}\left(t_{0}<\eta(x)\leq t_{1}\right)\\
y\mathbb{I}\left(t_{1}<\eta(x)\leq t_{2}\right)-\theta_{2}\mathbb{I}\left(t_{1}<\eta(x)\leq t_{2}\right)\\
y\mathbb{I}\left(t_{2}<\eta(x)\leq t_{3}\right)-\theta_{3}\mathbb{I}\left(t_{2}<\eta(x)\leq t_{3}\right)\\
\mathbb{I}\left(\eta(x)\leq t_{1}\right)-\frac{1}{3}\\
\mathbb{I}\left(\eta(x)\leq t_{2}\right)-\frac{2}{3}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, the final estimators
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\rm Frac}j}
are averages over split-specific estimators as in
eq.

3.2
.

Note that the conditions in
3.1
are met whenever
{\eta^{*}_{P}}(x)
is not flat in
x
.
This condition is testable, for example using the one-sided test for the accuracy in
Figure

1
.

B.4.3
Monte Carlo Designs

I simulate outcome and covariates by (i) converting each observed column to rank-based uniforms
U=\text{rank}(X)/(n+1)
, (ii) Gaussianizing to
Z=\Phi^{-1}(U)
and estimating the latent normal correlation
\Sigma^{*}
, (iii) drawing
Z^{\ast}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{*})
and mapping back to uniforms
U^{\ast}=\Phi(Z^{\ast})
, and (iv) inverting each margin with the empirical CDF of the corresponding variable.
For the correlated design, I modify
\Sigma^{*}
by multiplying by 3 the first row/column, the one corresponding to the correlation between outcome and covariates, and use as correlation matrix its nearest positive definite matrix in case the modified
\Sigma^{*}
is no longer positive definite.
For the uncorrelated design, I sample covariates the same way, and the outcome is sampled independently from a binomial distribution with probability
0.07
.

Comparison of Top-Bottom Estimates

Figure

5
compares the top minus bottom estimates across datasets and methods, similar to
Figure

1
.

B.5.2
Monte Carlo Designs

The designs are explicitly calibrated to the observed data so that simulated covariates and outcomes are distributionally aligned with the original sample.

Treatment assignment.

I draw the treatment assignment indicator from a Bernoulli distribution with mean
0.5
.

Covariates and potential outcome under control.

Starting from the observed outcome and covariate matrix for the control sample, I form pseudo-uniforms for each column by ranking within sample and scaling,
U=\mathrm{rank}(X)/(n{+}1)
.
I then Gaussianize to
Z=\Phi^{-1}(U)
and estimate the latent normal correlation
\Sigma^{*}
on these
Z
(taking the nearest positive definite matrix if needed).
To generate synthetic
Y(0)
and covariates, I draw
Z^{\ast}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{*})
, map to uniforms
U^{\ast}=\Phi(Z^{\ast})
, and invert each margin via the empirical CDF of the corresponding original variable.

Treatment effect.

From the original data, I estimate two arm-specific components as functions of treatment and covariates.
The first is a logistic regression for whether
Y=0
(no donation), using treatment, covariates and their interactions.
The second is a Poisson regression, with amount of donation as outcome and same variables in the model.
For generating simulated observations, the treatment effect is zero with probability
q_{0}(x,y_{0})-q_{1}(x,y_{0})
(rounded to zero or one if necessary), where

q_{d}(x,y_{0})=(1-\pi_{d}(x))\;\hat{P}(Y\geq y_{0}{+}1\mid X{=}x,D{=}d),

with
x
being the covariates,
y_{0}
the value of potential outcome under control,
\pi_{d}(x)
the probability that
Y=0
coming from the logit model with coefficients associated with treatment
=1
being multiplied by 4, and
\hat{P}(Y\geq y_{0}{+}1\mid X{=}x,D{=}d)
coming from the Poisson model with mean multiplied by
0.05
.
Conditional on the treatment effect being different from zero, I draw
Y(1)
from a truncated Poisson distribution starting at
Y(0)
with the same mean coming from the Poisson regression.

Final outcome.

For the design where treatment effect heterogeneity is predictable, I generate the observed outcome as
Y(1)
if treatment is
1
, and
Y(0)
otherwise.
For the design where treatment effect heterogeneity is not predictable, I generate the entire dataset exactly the same way, but shuffle the treatment assignment indicator at random as the last step.

gives the number of Monte Carlo iterations used for each specification.

B.5.4
Theoretical Properties of Ensemble Approach

I establish the theoretical properties of the ensemble estimator using the CLTs proven in this paper.
I show that when there is detectable heterogeneity, i.e., when the ensemble weights
({\hat{\beta}}_{a})_{a=1}^{A}
do not converge to zero, the confidence interval based on the normal approximation is asymptotically exact.
If there is no detectable heterogeneity, however, my theoretical result gives no coverage guarantee to the normal approximation CI.
Extensive simulation exercises, including but not limited to those of
Section

7
, suggest that the normal approximation CI is actually conservative under the null hypothesis of no heterogeneity for small values of
A
and
K
such as
A=4
and
K=3
.
Hence, my recommendation for empirical practice is to use the normal approximation CI with no more than 4 algorithms and 5 folds.
I also propose an adaptive approach using ideas developed in
Section

4.2
that is valid even when there is no detectable heterogeneity, at the cost of having smaller power.

First, I introduce additional notation.
Denote the set of splits

\mathcal{S}=\left(\mathsf{s}_{m,k}\right)_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]},

and the set of model
{\hat{\eta}}=({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}
.
I use
F_{P}(x)
to denote the cdf of the random variable
\sum_{a=1}^{A}{\beta^{*}_{P}}_{a}{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}_{a}(X)
and

F_{P}^{-1}(p)=\inf\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}:p\leq F_{P}(x)\right\}.

For some results, I focus on a set
\mathcal{P}_{hte}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
such that
\left(F_{P}^{-1}(t)\right)_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{hte}}
is equicontinuous at points
t=j/J
for
j=1,\dots,J
.
This is a collection of DGPs where the
J
quantiles of the limit predicted ITE
\sum_{a=1}^{A}{\beta^{*}_{P}}_{a}{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}_{a}(X)
are well-defined.
This is required so that the groups defined in
eq.

7.6
are well-defined in the limit.
Note that
F_{P}^{-1}(j/J)
being continuous implies that the limit predictor
\sum_{a=1}^{A}{\beta^{*}_{P}}_{a}{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}_{a}(X)
is not flat in
X
, so this class essentially excludes DGPs where there is no detectable heterogeneity, that is, where the true CATE
\eta_{P}(x)
is flat in
x
.

My first result is that the normal approximation CI is asymptotically exact when there is detectable heterogeneity.
It relies on
B.5
, defined in
Section

B.5.6
.
It is a mild but technical assumption that requires: (i) the weights
{\hat{\beta}}_{\ell,a}
have finite limits, (ii) a standard moments condition, (iii) propensity scores are bounded away from
0
and
1
, (iv) the variance-covariance matrix of the regressors
Z
is positive definite, and (v) the models estimated with ML converge to any limit at any rate.

Theorem B.3
.

Let
B.5
hold, and let
\mathcal{P}_{hte}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be such that
\left(F_{P}^{-1}(t)\right)_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{hte}}
is equicontinuous at points
t=j/J
for
j=1,\dots,J
.
Then, for any sequence
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{hte}
,

P_{n}\left(\delta_{\hat{\eta}}\in\left[\hat{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}},\hat{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}}+z_{1-\alpha/2}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right]\right)\to 1-\alpha.

∎

Although
B.3
does not cover cases when there is no detectable heterogeneity, extensive simulation exercises, including but not limited to the ones of
Section

7
, suggest that the coverage probability is larger than
1-\alpha
in those cases at least when
A\leq 4
,
K\leq 5
, that is, the CI
B.3
is conservative.
Next, I consider a test for detectable heterogeneity that can be used, for example, when
A>4
and/or
K>5
.
If the test rejects no detectable heterogeneity, the normal approximation CI may be used.

B.5.5
A Test for Detectable Heterogeneity

I propose using a version of the test proposed in
Section

4.2.1
for testing whether the models
{\hat{\eta}}=({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{m,k}})
have explanatory power for heterogeneous treatment effects.
Specifically, I first calculate the mean squared of residuals from the BLP regression

Y_{i}=\alpha_{1}+\sum_{a=1}^{A}\beta_{a}({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}},a}(X_{i})-\bar{\tau}_{\mathsf{s},a})\left[T_{i}-p(X_{i})\right]+\alpha_{2}Z_{i}+\varepsilon_{i},\qquad i\in\mathsf{s}

(B.25)

with weights
\omega_{i}=\left\{p(X_{i})\left[1-p(X_{i})\right]\right\}^{-1}
,
\bar{\tau}_{\mathsf{s},a}=|s|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}},a}(X_{i})
, for
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, as in
eq.

7.5
but at the fold level.
Denote it by

MSR_{\mathsf{s}}=\frac{1}{|s|}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\left(Y_{i}-\hat{\alpha}_{1,\mathsf{s}}+\sum_{a=1}^{A}\hat{\beta}_{a,\mathsf{s}}({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}},a}(X_{i})-\bar{\tau}_{\mathsf{s},a})\left[T_{i}-p(X_{i})\right]+\hat{\alpha}_{2,\mathsf{s}}Z_{i}\right)^{2}

I compare
(MSR_{\mathsf{s}})_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}
with

MSR_{b}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(Y_{i}-\hat{\alpha}_{1,b}+\hat{\alpha}_{2,b}Z_{i}\right)^{2},

where
\hat{\alpha}_{1,b}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{2,b}
are the estimates from the weighted least squares regression

Y_{i}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}Z_{i}+\varepsilon_{i},\qquad i\in{1,\dots,n}.

Let
\hat{\Sigma}
be an estimate of the asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{n}\left(MSR_{\mathsf{s}}-MSR_{b}\right)_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}
, and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\mathsf{s}}
are the entries of the main diagonal.
I propose calculating the test-statistic

\hat{T}=\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\min\left\{\sqrt{n}\frac{MSR_{\mathsf{s}}-MSR_{b}}{\hat{\sigma}_{\mathsf{s}}},0\right\}\right)^{2}.

I establish the validity of this test in
B.4
, where
\hat{c}_{1-\alpha}
is calculated as in
Section

4.2.1
.
The result follows from
4.2
.

Theorem B.4
.

Let
B.5
hold, and let

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\left\{P\in\mathcal{P}:\exists c\in\mathbb{R},\eta_{P}(x)=c\right\}.

Then, for any sequence
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{0}
,

P_{n}\left(\hat{T}>\hat{c}_{1-\alpha}\right)\to 1-\alpha.

∎

Denote the normal approximation CI

\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,\mathcal{N}}=\left[\hat{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}},\hat{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}}+z_{1-\alpha/2}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right],

and the extended CI

\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,{\rm ext}}=\operatorname{Conv}\left(\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,\mathcal{N}}\cup\{0\}\right),

where
\operatorname{Conv}
denotes the convex hull, that is,
\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,{\rm ext}}
has all the elements in
\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,\mathcal{N}}
,
0
, and all elements in between.
For a given fixed
\bar{c}_{5}\geq 0
, denote the final CI

\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,\mathcal{N}},\text{ if }\hat{T}^{\prime}>\hat{c}_{1-\alpha}\\
\widehat{\rm CI}_{\alpha,{\rm ext}},\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

B.4
implies that this CI is asymptotically valid pointwise in
P\in\mathcal{P}
for
\bar{c}_{5}=0
, and uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
for any
\bar{c}_{5}>0
.

B.5.6
Proofs and Extra Definitions

Define
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}
as the space that contains the covariates
X\in\mathcal{X}
for some integer
d_{x}>0
.
Let
Y^{T}=(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
.
For any

d=(d_{j,k})_{j\in[J],k\in[K]},

\beta=(\beta_{\ell,a})_{\ell\in[L],a\in[A]},

and
\eta\in H
,
let

H_{\eta,\beta,d}^{T}=\left(Z_{i},\left[\left\{T_{i}-p(X_{i})\right\}\mathbb{I}\left(d_{j-1,k(i)}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{A}\beta_{\ell(i),a}\eta_{a}(X_{i})<d_{j,k(i)}\right)\right]_{j=1}^{J}\right)_{i=1}^{n}.

H_{{\hat{\eta}},\beta,d}^{T}=\left(Z_{i},\left[\left\{T_{i}-p(X_{i})\right\}\mathbb{I}\left(d_{j-1,k(i)}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{A}\beta_{\ell(i),a}{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{k(i)},a}(X_{i})<d_{j,k(i)}\right)\right]_{j=1}^{J}\right)_{i=1}^{n}.

\Omega
is the n-by-n diagonal matrix of weights:

\Omega=\operatorname{diag}(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}).

{\beta^{*}_{P}}_{\ell,a}
is the coefficient of the linear projection with weights
\omega
of
Y
on

\left[\left\{T_{i}-p(X_{i})\right\}({\eta^{*}_{P}}_{a}(X)-\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{{\eta^{*}_{P}}_{a}(X)}\right])\right]_{j=1}^{J}

when that is well-defined, and zero otherwise.
Note
{\beta^{*}_{P}}_{\ell,a}
is the same for all
\ell
since the limit
{\eta^{*}_{P}}_{a}
does not depend on the data.
Let
F_{P}(x)
be the cdf of the random variable
\sum_{a=1}^{A}{\beta^{*}_{P}}_{a}{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}_{a}(X)
and

F_{P}^{-1}(p)=\inf\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}:p\leq F_{P}(x)\right\}.

Define

{d^{*}_{P}}_{j,k}=F_{P}^{-1}(j/J).

Similarly,
{d^{*}_{P}}_{j,k}
is the same for all
k
.

\hat{d}=(\hat{d}_{j,k})_{j\in[J],k\in[K]},

{\hat{\beta}}=({\hat{\beta}}_{\ell,a})_{\ell\in[L],a\in[A]}.

Define
\theta_{\eta,\beta,d}
and column vector
\varepsilon_{\eta,\beta,d}
such that

Y=H_{\eta,\beta,d}\theta_{\eta,\beta,d}+\varepsilon_{\eta,\beta,d}.

(B.26)

\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{(m)}=(\hat{\alpha},\left(\hat{\gamma}_{j=1}^{J}\right))^{T}
are the estimates from
eq.

7.7
, and

\theta_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{(m)}
denotes
{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}
from the
m
-th repetition.

Assumption B.5
.

The following conditions hold:

(i)

For some
B=(B_{\beta}\times B_{d})\subset\mathbb{R}^{LA}\times\mathbb{R}^{JK}
with compact
B_{\beta}
,

\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}(\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P})\subseteq B;

(ii)

For some
\bar{c}_{6}>0
,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{\eta\in H,(\beta,d)\in B}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|H_{\eta,\beta,d,i}^{T}\varepsilon_{\eta,\beta,d,i}\right|^{2+\bar{c}_{6}}}\right]<\infty;

(iii)

For some
\bar{c}_{7}>0
, and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\bar{c}_{7}<p(x)<1-\bar{c}_{7};

(iv)

\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\det\left(\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[Z\right]\right)>0
.

(v)

There exists
({\eta^{*}_{P}}_{a})_{a=1}^{A}
such that

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|{\tilde{\eta}}_{a}(X)-{\eta^{*}_{P}}_{a}(X)\right||D}\right]\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where
{\tilde{\eta}}_{a}=\mathcal{A}_{a}(D)
and
X\perp D
.

∎

Theorem B.5
.

Let
B.5
hold, and
\mathcal{P}_{hte}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be such that
\left(F_{P}^{-1}(t)\right)_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{hte}}
is equicontinuous at points
t=j/J
for
j=1,\dots,J
.
Then,

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}\right)-\sqrt{n}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}^{T}\Omega H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}}\right]^{-1}H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}_{hte}
.

∎

Proof of
B.5
.

First, note that equicontinuity of
\left(F_{P}^{-1}(t)\right)_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{hte}}
implies the
J
quantiles groups to be well-defined, which together with
B.5
implies

\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{hte}}\det\left(H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}^{T}\Omega H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}\right)>0.

For each
m=1,\dots,M
, using
eq.

B.26
leads to the decomposition

\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{(m)}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{(m)}=\left(H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}\right)^{-1}H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}.

\left(n^{-1}H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}\right)^{-1}\xrightarrow{P}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}^{T}\Omega H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}}\right]^{-1}

by a uniform law of large numbers.
The terms in
n^{-1/2}H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}
are given by

\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\varepsilon_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d},i}Z_{i},\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\varepsilon_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d},i}\left[T_{i}-p(X_{i})\right]\mathbb{I}\left(\hat{d}_{j-1,k(i)}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{A}\beta_{\ell(i),a}{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{k(i)},a}(X_{i})<\hat{d}_{j,k(i)}\right)\right\}_{j=1}^{J}\right).

These are split-sample empirical processes as in
E.1
, with functions

f_{(\beta,d),\eta,1}(y,h,\omega)=\omega(y-h^{T}\theta_{\eta,\beta,d})z

and

f_{(\beta,d),\eta,1+j}(y,h,k,\ell,\omega)=\omega(y-h^{T}\theta_{\eta,\beta,d})(t-p(x))\mathbb{I}\left(d_{j-1,k}\leq\sum_{a=1}^{A}\beta_{\ell,a}{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{k},a}(X_{i})<\hat{d}_{j,k}\right).

Step one of the proof of
E.1
gives

\sup_{(\beta,d)\in B}\left\lVert n^{-1/2}H_{{\hat{\eta}},\beta,d}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\hat{\eta}},\beta,d}-n^{-1/2}H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta,d}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta,d}\right\rVert\xrightarrow{P}0.

Together with consistency of
({\hat{\beta}},\hat{d})
to
(\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P})
, which follows from a uniform law of large numbers, this gives

n^{-1/2}H_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}=n^{-1/2}H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}+o_{P}(1).

Finally, asymptotic equicontinuity in
(\beta,d)
gives

n^{-1/2}H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\hat{\beta}},\hat{d}}=n^{-1/2}H_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}^{T}\Omega\varepsilon_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\beta^{*}_{P},d^{*}_{P}}+o_{P}(1).

Summing over
m\in M
concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
B.3
.

Follows from
B.5
, Lyapunov’s CLT and consistency of
\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}}
, which follows by a law of large numbers.
∎

Proof of
B.4
.

Follows directly from
4.2
, noting that

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{MSR_{\mathsf{s}}|\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right]\geq\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{MSR_{b}}\right]

always holds when
\eta_{0,P}(x)
is flat, since in that case the true coefficients
(\beta_{a})
in regression
eq.

B.25
are all zero.
∎

Appendix C
Modeling Power

I formalize the notion that using a larger sample for training is desirable by the analyst by introducing the concept of modeling power.
This appendix uses notation introduced in
Section

2
.
I say that an estimator has better modeling power than another if its collection of splits has a smaller expected loss.
Although my results rely on no assumptions on the training algorithm
\mathcal{A}
other than a mild stability condition on
\mathcal{A}(D)
, in practice,
\mathcal{A}
typically minimizes some loss function.
For example, in
2
, logistic regression minimizes log-likelihood, and neural networks minimize classification error over a class of network architectures.
Let
{\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\left({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{m,k}}\right)_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]}
,
\ell_{\eta}(W)
be a loss function,

\phi(\eta)=\int\ell_{\eta}(w)dP(w)

be the loss value of function
\eta
, and

\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}})=(MK)^{-1}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}).

Note that
\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}})
is equal to the expected value of
\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\mathsf{s}})
over
\mathsf{s}\in\left(\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{m,k}\right)_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]}
uniformly at random, which is equivalent to the loss value of using a function
{\hat{\eta}}
that takes value in
{\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}}
uniformly at random.
The expected loss is defined as
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}})}\right]
.

The expected loss, and thus the modeling power of an estimator depends only on the sample size used to estimate the functions in
{\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
That is because

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}})}\right]=(MK)^{-1}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{s\in r}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})}\right]=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\xi})}\right],

where
\xi
is a random subset of
\left[n\right]
of size
n-b
, with
b=n/K
if
K>1
, and assuming that
n
is a multiple of
K
for simplicity.
If
{\hat{\eta}}_{\xi}
is calculated with the goal of minimizing the loss
\phi(\eta)
with respect to
\eta
, it is reasonable to assume that the expected loss
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\phi({\hat{\eta}}_{\xi})}\right]
decreases with the sample size used to calculate
{\hat{\eta}}_{\xi}
.
If that is the case, the expected loss increases with
b
, since fewer data are used to estimate each
{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{m,k}}
.
Hence, to increase modeling power when
K=1
, one can pick a smaller
b
(and
\pi
).
However, if
\bar{M}<\infty
, a smaller
b
leads to smaller statistical power, since fewer data are used as evaluation sample at each split.
When using cross-fitting, modeling power increases with
K
, since
b=n/K
.
In this case, the returns to increasing
K
are diminishing.
For example, if
K=2
,
{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{m,k}}
is calculated with
50
% of the sample, and this fraction raises to
90
% with
K=10
.
If
K=20
, however, the fraction only raises by another
5
%.
Although a large value of
K
or small value of
\pi
(when
K=1
) lead to better modelling power, my asymptotic framework takes these quantities as fixed.
This means that the quality of the asymptotic approximation may be poor if
K
is large (or
\pi
small) relative to the sample size.
For example, my asymptotic framework does not accommodate for leave-one-out cross-fitting, that is,
K=n
.

Appendix D
CLT for Split-Sample Averages

I derive a CLT for split-sample estimators based on sample averages.
The objective is to expose my main result in an accessible setting, and discuss the main insights of the proof.
The result is generalized in
Appendix

E
, where I derive a functional CLT uniformly over a large set of data generating processes, and in
Section

3
where I prove a CLT for Z-estimators.

The notation follows
Section

2
.
Additionally, let
f_{\eta}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable functions for
\eta\in H
, and define

Pf_{\eta}=\int_{w}f_{\eta}(w)dP(w),

(D.1)

that is,
Pf_{\eta}
is a marginal expectation that takes
\eta
as fixed.
This is typical notation in the empirical process literature.

Example
3

(Revisited)
.

In the probabilistic classifiers example,
W=(Y,X)
,
\eta
is a function that predicts the probability of
Y=1
given
X
, and

f_{\eta}(w)=\eta(x)\mathbb{I}\left(y=1\right)+(1-\eta(x))\mathbb{I}\left(y=0\right).

Pf_{\eta}
is the correct classification rate of predictor
\eta
.

∎

In this section, I consider estimators of the form

\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i}),

(D.2)

where
\mathcal{R}
is a collection of
M
random splits or cross-splits of the sample,
K
is the number of folds (
K=1
denotes sample-splitting),
b
is the size of each subsample
\mathsf{s}
(either the chosen subsample size when
K=1
or the approximate fold size
n/K
when
K>1
), and
{\hat{\eta}}={\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\left(\left({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\right)_{r\in\mathcal{R}}
.
I show in
D.1
that
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}
is
\sqrt{n}
-Gaussian when centered around its marginal expectation

\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}=P\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}=\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}.

In
3
,
\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}
is the fraction of individuals correctly classified under a rule that predicts
Y=1
with probability

\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(x)

for an individual with characteristics
X=x
.

D.1
establishes sufficient conditions for the CLT in Theorem
D.1
.

Assumption D.1
.

(i)

For some
\delta>0
,

\sup_{\eta\in H}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|f_{\eta}(W)\right|^{2+\delta}}\right]<\infty.

(ii)

For some
\eta^{*}\in H
and
{\tilde{\eta}}=\mathcal{A}(D)
,

f_{\tilde{\eta}}(w)\xrightarrow{P}f_{\eta^{*}}(w)

pointwise for every
w
.

∎

D.1(i)
is a standard moments condition for CLTs, uniformly over possible values of
\eta
.

D.1(ii)
is a mild stability condition on
{\tilde{\eta}}
.
Importantly,
{\tilde{\eta}}
is allowed to converge at any rate and to any limit
\eta^{*}
.
This condition is more interpretable but stronger than what I use for proving the more general CLTs in
Appendices

E
and
3
.

D.1(ii)
differs from the typical approach in the double machine learning literature where faster convergence rates (often
n^{-1/4}
) are required for nuisance functions, in a context where the target parameter does not depend on the estimated model
{\hat{\eta}}

(
13
, e.g.,)
.

Theorem D.1
.

Let
D.1
hold.
Then,

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right)\leadsto\mathcal{N}\left(0,V_{\bar{M},K}P\left(f_{\eta^{*}}-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)^{2}\right),

where

V_{\bar{M},K}=\begin{cases}\bar{M}^{-1}\left(\pi^{-1}+\bar{M}-1\right),&\text{if }K=1\text{ and }\bar{M}<\infty\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

∎

D.1
can be used to construct confidence intervals with the standard error

\hat{\sigma}_{\hat{\eta}}=\sqrt{V_{M,K}}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}},

where

\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})-\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})\right)^{2}

and

V_{M,K}=\begin{cases}M^{-1}\left(n/b+M-1\right),&\text{if }K=1\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Theorem D.2
.

Let
D.1
hold and
P\left(f_{\eta^{*}}-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)^{2}>0
.
Then,

P\left(\theta_{\hat{\eta}}\in\left[\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}-z_{1-\alpha/2}\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{\hat{\eta}}}{\sqrt{n}},\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}+z_{1-\alpha/2}\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{\hat{\eta}}}{\sqrt{n}}\right]\right)\to 1-\alpha.

∎

The proof of
D.1
relies on four main insights.
I show them for the case of repeated cross-fitting, assuming that
n
is a multiple of
K
for simplicity.
I provide a more detailed proof in
Section

D.1
, and a formal proof follows from the more general
E.1
and
3.1
.
The first insight and main argument of the proof is to show that

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right)=\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)+o_{P}(1).

(D.3)

Once this is established, the result follows from Lyapunov’s CLT, since
\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{n}
are iid.
The second insight is that an application of Markov and Hölder inequalities gives that a sufficient condition for
eq.

D.3
is that

\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left[f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]-\left[f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right]\right]\to 0,

(D.4)

where
\xi
is a random subset of
\left[n\right]
of size
b=n/K
and
\tilde{\xi}
is its complement.
The third insight is that an application of the Law of Total Variance gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left[f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]-\left[f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

(D.5)

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W)-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right].

(D.6)

Since the summands in
eq.

D.5
are iid conditional on
D_{\tilde{\xi}}
,
eq.

D.5
equals
eq.

D.6
, which does not rely on the term
\sqrt{b}
.
This is the crucial step that enables asymptotic normality without requiring an assumption on the rate at which
f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)
converges to
f_{\eta^{*}}(W)
.

The final insight is that
D.1
gives a sufficient condition for
eq.

D.6
to converge to zero.
For any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W)-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|\leq\varepsilon\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)}\right],

where the first term is bounded by
\varepsilon^{2}
.
By Hölder’s inequality, the second term is bounded by

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|^{2+\delta}}\right]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)^{\frac{\delta}{2+\delta}}.

The first term above is bounded by
D.1(i)
, and the second term can be made arbitrarily small since

\displaystyle P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\Bigm|W\right)}\right]

converges to zero by the dominated convergence theorem, since

P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\Bigm|W=w\right)=P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\right)\to 0

from
D.1(ii)
and independence of
W
and
{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}
.
The result follows since
\varepsilon
can be made arbitrarily small.

D.1
Proofs

Proof of
D.1
.

I provide a detailed proof for the repeated cross-fitting case discussed in
Appendix

D
, since that contains the main insights of the proof.
A complete and formal proof follows from the more general
E.1
.

The argument consists of showing that

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right)=\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)+o_{P}(1)

and applying Lyapunov’s CLT to the first term on the right side of the equality.

Define
h(w,\eta)=\left[f_{\eta}(w)-Pf_{\eta}\right]-\left[f_{\eta^{*}}(w)-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right]
and note that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}-\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right)-\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{bK}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}),

since
b=n/K
for cross-fitting.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle P\left(\left|\sqrt{bK}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\frac{\sqrt{K}}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{K}}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})\right|}\right]

(D.7)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{K}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right|}\right]

(D.8)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{K}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right|^{2}}\right]^{1/2}

(D.9)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{K}\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right]^{1/2}.

(D.10)

eq.

D.7
follows from Markov’s inequality.

eq.

D.8
defines
\xi
as a random subset of
\left[n\right]
of size
b
, and uses the fact that the expected value does not depend on how the sample is (randomly) split.

eq.

D.9
follows from Hölder’s inequality.

eq.

D.10
follows since

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h(W,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})}\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W)-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=0

by definition.

Since
K
is assumed fixed, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

(D.11)

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W)-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

(D.12)

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

(D.13)

converges to zero.

eq.

D.11
follows from the Law of Total Variance, since

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{\eta^{*}}(W_{i})-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]=0.

eq.

D.12
follows since the observations are iid conditional on
D_{\tilde{\xi}}
.

To show convergence to zero of
eq.

D.13
, consider the inequality

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}}\right].

For any fixed
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|\leq\varepsilon\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)}\right].

The first term is bounded by
\varepsilon^{2}
.
By Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|^{2+\delta}}\right]^{\frac{2}{2+\delta}}P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)^{\frac{\delta}{2+\delta}}.

The first term above is bounded by
D.1(i)
, and the second term can be made arbitrarily small since

\displaystyle P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\right)=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|>\varepsilon\Bigm|W\right)}\right]

converges to zero by the dominated convergence theorem, since

P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\Bigm|W=w\right)=P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\right)\to 0

from
D.1(ii)
and independence of
W
and
{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}
.
The result follows since
\varepsilon
can be made arbitrarily small.
∎

Proof of
D.2
.

Note

\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\left(f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})^{2}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})\right)^{2}.

By a law of large numbers conditional on
\tilde{\mathsf{s}}
,

\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})^{2}-\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)^{2}|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]\xrightarrow{P}0,

and similarly

\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W_{i})-\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]\xrightarrow{P}0.

Hence,

\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}^{2}-\left(\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)^{2}|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]-\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]^{2}\right)\xrightarrow{P}0.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and define
h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(w)=\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\right|
.

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)\mathbb{I}\left(h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)\leq\varepsilon\right)|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]+\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)\mathbb{I}\left(h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)>\varepsilon\right)|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)^{1+\delta}|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1+\delta}}P\left(h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)>\varepsilon|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)^{\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}}

by Hölder’s inequality.
The term
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)^{1+\delta}|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]^{\frac{1}{1+\delta}}
is bounded by
D.1(i)
, and I show that
P\left(h_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)>\varepsilon|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)^{\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}}
converges in probability to zero.
In the proof of
D.1
, I established that

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)}\right]=P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\right)\to 0.

This implies that
P\left(\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(w)-f_{\eta^{*}}(w)\right|>\varepsilon\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\right)\xrightarrow{P}0

since
L_{1}
convergence implies convergence in probability.
Hence,

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left|f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)-f_{\eta}^{*}(W)\right||D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]\xrightarrow{P}0,

which implies

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]-\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{\eta}^{*}(W)}\right]\xrightarrow{P}0.

A similar argument gives

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(W)^{2}|D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right]-\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{f_{\eta}^{*}(W)^{2}}\right]\xrightarrow{P}0.

Combining results implies

\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}^{2}\xrightarrow{P}P\left(f_{\eta^{*}}-Pf_{\eta^{*}}\right)^{2}.

The result follows from
D.1
, since
V_{M,K}/V_{\bar{M},K}\to 1
.
∎

Appendix E
CLT for Split-Sample Empirical Processes

I derive a CLT for empirical processes based on a broad class of split-sample procedures, uniformly over a large class of probability distributions.
This section generalizes
Appendix

D
, which gives a more accessible exposition focusing on the particular case of sample averages.
The CLT of this section can be used to prove asymptotic normality for a large class of estimators.
That is the case for Z-estimators, which I develop in
Section

3
.
Moreover, this CLT can be used to establish asymptotic consistency of the bootstrap in several applications, following, for example, the arguments in Chapter 3.7 of
49
.

The notation follows
Section

2
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a set of probability distributions, and
D=\{W_{i}\}_{i\in\left[n\right]}
, the dataset, be an iid sample of
W\sim P\in\mathcal{P}
.
I denote the expected value under
P\in\mathcal{P}
by
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}
, and the variance by
\operatorname{Var}_{P}
.
Given a set
T
, let
f_{t,\eta}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable functions for
t\in T
and
\eta\in H
, with
H
defined as in
Section

2
, and let
\mathcal{F}_{\eta}=\left\{f_{t,\eta}:t\in T\right\}
.

{\hat{\eta}}={\hat{\eta}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\left({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{m,k}}\right)_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]}
,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{Q,r}=\left(\int|f|^{r}dQ\right)^{1/r}
,
L_{r}(Q)=\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{Q,r}
, and
\mathcal{Q}
denotes all finitely discrete probability distributions.
I use
\left|x\right|
to denote cardinality when
x
is a set and absolute value when
x
is scalar.
I denote by
N
and
N_{[\,]}
respectively the covering and bracketing numbers, as in Definitions 2.1.5 and 2.1.6 of
49
.
For
\mathsf{s}\subseteq\left[n\right]
, define the empirical measure

\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{s}}f_{t,\eta}=\frac{1}{|\mathsf{s}|}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f_{t,\eta}(W_{i}),

the marginal expectation

Pf_{t,\eta}=\int_{w}f_{t,\eta}(w)dP(w),

and the empirical process

\mathbb{G}_{n,{\hat{\eta}}}(t)=\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{s}}f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}-Pf_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right).

I establish below sufficient conditions for the CLT for split-sample empirical processes, presented in
E.1
.

Assumption E.1
.

The following conditions hold:

(i)

T
is totally bounded for some semimetric
\rho
;

(ii)

For every
\eta\in H
and
t\in T
,
f_{t,\eta}
is measurable;

(iii)

For all
\eta\in H
, there exists a measurable envelope function
F_{\eta}
;
That is,
F_{\eta}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
is such that
|f_{t,\eta}(w)|\leq F_{\eta}(w)<\infty
for all
t\in T
and
w\in\mathcal{W}
;

(iv)

\lim_{B\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{\eta\in H}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{F_{\eta}(W)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(F_{\eta}(W)>B\right)}\right]=0
;

(v)

For every
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{\eta\in H}\sup_{\rho(t,t^{\prime})<\delta_{n}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\left(f_{t,\eta}(W)-f_{t^{\prime},\eta}(W)\right)^{2}}\right]\to 0;

(vi)

One of the following conditions holds for all
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
:

\sup_{\eta\in H}\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\int_{0}^{\delta_{n}}\sqrt{\log N\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\eta},L_{2}(Q)\right)}d\varepsilon\to 0,

(E.1)

or

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{\eta\in H}\int_{0}^{\delta_{n}}\sqrt{\log N_{[\,]}\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\eta},L_{2}(P)\right)}d\varepsilon\to 0;

(E.2)

∎

Assumption E.2
.

There exists
{\eta^{*}_{P}}\in H
such that for
{\tilde{\eta}}=\mathcal{A}(D)
,
W\perp D
, and every
t\in T
,

\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[f_{t,{\tilde{\eta}}}(W)-f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W)\Bigm|D\right]\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

∎

Although technical,
E.1
is a weak condition that is satisfied in many applications.

E.1(i)
through
E.1(vi)
are standard Donsker conditions in the literature of weak convergence of empirical processes
(
49
, e.g.,)
, generalized for the presence of the functions
\eta\in H
. In fact, if
T=\left\{t\right\}
and
\mathcal{P}=\left\{P\right\}
are singletons, these conditions are implied by the “
2+\delta
” moments condition in
D.1(i)
(
H.1
).
These assumptions are standard for proving functional CLTs by limiting the complexity of the sets
T
and
\mathcal{F}_{\eta}
.
In addition to ensuring that each set
\mathcal{F}_{\eta}
is Donsker,
E.1
requires that the inequalities and convergences be uniform in
\eta\in H
.
Importantly,
E.1(vi)
does not restrict the complexity of the class
H
, and it does not imply the much stronger condition that
\bigcup_{\eta\in H}\mathcal{F}_{\eta}
is Donsker.
In applications, except for the restrictions on
\mathcal{P}
,
E.1(i)
through
E.1(vi)
are verifiable since they depend only on the choices of
T
and
\mathcal{F}_{\eta}
, and typically do not depend on how
\eta
is calculated.
The assumptions on
\mathcal{P}
involve the mild uniform square integrability condition
E.1(iv)
, and the smoothness condition
E.1(v)
.

Assumptions
E.1(i)
through
E.1(vi)
give standard conditions for a CLT when
\mathcal{R}
consists of a single sample split.
The proof for the case of multiple splits relies on the additional
E.2
.
This is a weak stability condition that requires
{\tilde{\eta}}
to converge at any rate to any function
{\eta^{*}_{P}}
, which is allowed to depend on
P
.
If
T
and
\mathcal{P}
are singletons, this is implied by
D.1(ii)
(
H.1
). Note that the requirement is pointwise in
t\in T
, and it holds, for example, if
f_{t,{\tilde{\eta}}}(w)\xrightarrow{P}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(w)
for almost all
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

Theorem E.1
.

(CLT for split-sample empirical processes)

Let
E.1
and
E.2
hold.
Then, the sequence
\mathbb{G}_{n,{\hat{\eta}}}
is asymptotically
\rho
-equicontinuous uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
and

\sup_{t\in T}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n,{\hat{\eta}}}(t)-\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t)\right|\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
, where

\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t)=\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{s}}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right).

For any sequence
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
such that, for every
t,t^{\prime}\in T
,

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W)-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)\left(f_{t^{\prime},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W)-P_{n}f_{t^{\prime},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)}\right]\to\sigma_{t,t^{\prime}},

(E.3)

for some
\sigma_{t,t^{\prime}}
,

\mathbb{G}_{n,{\hat{\eta}}}\leadsto\mathbb{G}_{\eta^{*}}

in
\ell^{\infty}(T)
, where
\mathbb{G}_{\eta^{*}}
is a tight Gaussian process.
Moreover, the covariance function of
\mathbb{G}_{\eta^{*}}
is given by
V_{\bar{M},K}\sigma_{t,t^{\prime}}
, where

V_{\bar{M},K}=\begin{cases}\bar{M}^{-1}\left(\pi^{-1}+\bar{M}-1\right),&\text{if }K=1\text{ and }\bar{M}<\infty\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

∎

To the best of my knowledge, this appears to be the first central limit theorem for empirical processes that average over multiple splits of the sample.
This result enables asymptotic inference for a large class of split-sample estimators.
For example, combined with the functional delta method, it immediately implies asymptotic normality of Hadamard differentiable functionals of the split-sample empirical measure

\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\mathbb{P}_{\mathsf{s}}f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}.

In
Section

3
, I use
E.1
as a building block to prove asymptotic normality of split-sample Z-estimators, a broad class that cover many if not most estimators used in practice, including the ones in
Section

6
.

E.1
Proofs

Lemma E.1
.

Let Assumptions
E.1(i)
through
E.1(vi)
hold,
(\eta_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq H
be a deterministic sequence,
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, and
s\subseteq\left[n\right]
be a random (uniformly) subset of
\left[n\right]
such that
|\mathsf{s}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Define

X_{n,s}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{s}|}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\left(f_{t,\eta_{n}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,\eta_{n}}\right)

Then, the sequence
X_{n,s}
is asymptotically
\rho
-equicontinuous.

Proof of
Lemma

E.1
.

The result follows from an application of Theorems 2.11.1 and 2.11.9 in
49
, respectively for when conditions
eqs.

E.1
and
E.2
hold.
