Their notation is adapted with
m_{n}=|\mathsf{s}|
,
\mathcal{F}=T
, and
Z_{ni}(t)=|\mathsf{s}|^{-1/2}f_{t,\eta_{n}}(W_{i})
, where it is implicit in the notation that
W_{i}\sim P_{n}
(alternatively, one could denote
W_{ni}
instead of
W_{i}
).
The presence of the suprema over
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
\eta\in H
guarantee that the conditions in those theorems hold for any sequences
(\eta_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

Let Assumptions
E.1(i)
through
E.1(vi)
hold, and
s\subseteq\left[n\right]
be a random (uniformly) subset such that
|\mathsf{s}|\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Define

X_{n,s,\eta}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathsf{s}|}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\left(f_{t,\eta}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,\eta}\right).

Then, the sequence
X_{n,s,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}
is asymptotically
\rho
-equicontinuous uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\eta,\delta}=\left\{f-g:f,g\in\mathcal{F}_{\eta},\rho(f,g)<\delta\right\}
and
\varepsilon>0
.

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},\delta}}>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\int_{D_{s},D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\mathbb{I}\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})}(D_{s})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}),\delta}}>\varepsilon\right)dP(D_{s},D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})

(E.4)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\int_{D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\left[\int_{D_{s}}\mathbb{I}\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})}(D_{s})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}),\delta}}>\varepsilon\right)dP(D_{s})\right]dP(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})

(E.5)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\int_{D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\sup_{\eta\in H}\left[\int_{D_{s}}\mathbb{I}\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,\eta}(D_{s})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{\eta,\delta}}>\varepsilon\right)dP(D_{s})\right]dP(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{\eta\in H}\left[\int_{D_{s}}\mathbb{I}\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,\eta}(D_{s})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{\eta,\delta}}>\varepsilon\right)dP(D_{s})\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{\eta\in H}P\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,\eta}(D_{s})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{\eta,\delta}}>\varepsilon\right),

where
eq.

E.4
makes explicit the dependence of
X_{n,s,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}
on the subsample
D_{s}
and of
{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}
on
D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}
, and
eq.

E.5
uses the fact that the split is random and
D_{s},D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}
are independent.

Hence, for an arbitrary
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
,
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},\delta_{n}}}>\varepsilon\right)\to 0
follows from

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\left\lVert X_{n,s,\eta_{n}}(D_{s})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{\eta_{n},\delta_{n}}}>\varepsilon\right)\to 0

for any deterministic
(\eta_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq H
, which is established in
Lemma

E.1
.
∎

Proof of
E.1
.

The proof is divided into three main steps.
First, I show that

\sup_{t\in T}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n,{\hat{\eta}}}(t)-\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t)\right|\xrightarrow{P}0

uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
Second, I show that
\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
is asymptotically
\rho
-equicontinuous.
Finally, I prove the Gaussian limit of
(\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t))_{t\in T^{\prime}}
for any finite
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
.

Step one.

Let
h_{t}(w,\eta)=\left[f_{t,\eta}(w)-Pf_{t,\eta}\right]-\left[f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(w)-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right]
,
\pi_{n}=b/n
, and fix
\varepsilon>0
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\sup_{t\in T}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n,{\hat{\eta}}}(t)-\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t)\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\sup_{t\in T}\left|\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\frac{1}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\sup_{t\in T}\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\sup_{t\in T}\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}}{b}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})\right|}\right]

(E.6)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\sup_{t\in T}\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}}{b}\sum_{i\in\xi}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right|}\right]

(E.7)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\pi_{n}^{-1/2}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\sup_{t\in T}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right|}\right],

(E.8)

where
eq.

E.6
follows from Markov’s inequality, and
eq.

E.7
defines
\xi
as a random subset of
\left[n\right]
(uniformly over all subsets).

Since
\pi_{n}\to\pi\in(0,1)
,
eq.

E.8
converges to zero if the term inside the expectation convergences in probability to zero uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}
, since it is uniformly integrable (by
E.1(iv)
).
This follows from stochastic equicontinuity of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})
(as a process indexed by
t\in T
) and pointwise convergence in
t
, by applying Theorem 22.9 in
19
.
Stochastic equicontinuity follows since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left[f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]-\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left[f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right]

is a sum of two stochastically equicontinuous processes, respectively by
Lemma

E.2
and
Lemma

E.1
.
For pointwise convergence, I show that the variance converges to zero, and note
h_{t}(w,\eta)
is mean zero by construction.
For an arbitrary
t\in T
,

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left(f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}}\sum_{i\in\xi}\left(f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right)\Biggm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

(E.9)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\left(f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-Pf_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right)-\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W)-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right)\Biggm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]

(E.10)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[f_{t,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W)\Biggm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right],

where
eq.

E.9
uses the Law of Total Variance and the fact that
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{h_{t}(W_{i},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}})|D_{\tilde{\xi}}}\right]=0
, and
eq.

E.10
follows since the summands are iid conditional on
D_{\tilde{\xi}}
.
Finally, the last term converges to zero from
E.2
.
Note that since
f_{t,\eta}
are uniformly square integrable by
E.1(iv)
, convergence in probability of the conditional variance implies its convergence in
L_{1}
.

Step two.

Let
\lambda_{i}=(\pi_{n}MK)^{-1}\left|\left\{s\in\left\{s_{m,k}\right\}_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]}:i\in\mathsf{s}\right\}\right|
and note that

\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right).

Let
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{i}\right)_{i\in\left[n\right]}
,
\mathcal{F}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\delta}=\left\{f-g:f,g\in\mathcal{F}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},\rho(f,g)<\delta\right\}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
.

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\left\lVert\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\delta_{n}}}>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{P\left(\left\lVert\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\delta_{n}}}>\varepsilon\Bigm|\lambda\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lambda:\lambda_{i}\leq\pi_{n}^{-1}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P\left(\left\lVert\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}},\delta_{n}}}>\varepsilon\Bigm|\lambda\right).

The last term converges to zero from asymptotic equicontinuity of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{n,i}\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-Pf_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)
for arbitrary sequences
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and
(\lambda_{n,i})_{n\geq 1,i\in\left[n\right]}
satisfying
\lambda_{n,i}\leq\pi_{n}^{-1}
for all
n,i
.
Asymptotic equicontinuity can be verified under
E.1
, for example, by applying Theorem 2.11.1 (when
eq.

E.1
holds) and Theorem 2.11.9 (when
eq.

E.2
holds) of
49
.
Their notation is adapted with
m_{n}=n
,
\mathcal{F}=T
, and
Z_{ni}(t)=n^{-1/2}\lambda_{n,i}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})
, where it is implicit in the notation that
W_{i}\sim P_{n}
(alternatively, one could denote
W_{ni}
instead of
W_{i}
).
For
\gamma>0
, note that

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\sup_{t\in T}\left(n^{-1/2}\lambda_{n,i}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\sup_{t\in T}\left|n^{-1/2}\lambda_{n,i}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})\right|>\gamma\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{n}^{-2}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\sup_{t\in T}\left(n^{-1/2}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}\left(\sup_{t\in T}\left|n^{-1/2}\pi_{n}^{-1}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})\right|>\gamma\right)}\right],

and

\displaystyle\left(n^{-1/2}\lambda_{n,i}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-n^{-1/2}\lambda_{n,i}f_{t^{\prime},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{n}^{-2}\left(n^{-1/2}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-n^{-1/2}f_{t^{\prime},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})\right)^{2},

for any
t,t^{\prime}\in T
,
n
, and
i\in\left[n\right]
.

Step three.

If
K>1
,
\lambda_{i}=1
for all
i
, and the Gaussian limit follows from Lindeberg’s CLT and the Cramér-Wold device, using
E.1(iv)
.

For
K=1
and
\bar{M}<\infty
, let

M_{i}=\left|\left\{s\in\left\{s_{m,k}\right\}_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]}:i\in\mathsf{s}\right\}\right|,

so
\lambda_{i}=(\pi_{n}M)^{-1}M_{i}
.

\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\mathbb{G}_{n,{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(t)\mid\lambda\right]=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W)-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}^{2}.

Without loss of generality, let
M=\bar{M}
.

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{\pi_{n}^{2}\bar{M}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{M}}j^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(M_{i}=j\right).

In
Lemma

H.1
, I show that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(M_{i}=j\right)\xrightarrow{P_{n}}\binom{\bar{M}}{j}\pi^{j}(1-\pi)^{\bar{M}-j}.

Hence,

\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{M}}j^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(M_{i}=j\right)\xrightarrow{P_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{M}}j^{2}\binom{\bar{M}}{j}\pi^{j}(1-\pi)^{\bar{M}-j}=\pi(1-\pi)\bar{M}+(\pi\bar{M})^{2},

since the sum in the right is the second moment of a binomial distribution with parameters
\bar{M}
and
\pi
.
Collecting the results,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}^{2}\xrightarrow{P_{n}}1+(1-\pi)\pi^{-1}M^{-1}.

The Gaussian limit follows from Lindeberg’s CLT conditional on
\lambda
and the dominated convergence theorem, and the Cramér-Wold device.

Finally, let
K=1
and
\bar{M}=\infty
.
I show that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)

(E.11)

converges to zero in
L_{2}
.
For the mean,

\displaystyle\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda_{i}-1\right)\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\left(\lambda_{1}-1\right)\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{1})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}\biggl[\left(\lambda_{1}-1\right)\underbrace{\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{1})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\Bigm|\lambda_{1}}\right]}_{=0}\biggr].

For the variance,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda_{i}-1\right)\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda_{i}-1\right)\left(f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right)\Biggm|\lambda\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right]\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\lambda_{i}-1\right)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W_{i})-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\right]\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\left(\lambda_{1}-1\right)^{2}}\right],

where the first equality follows since
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W)-P_{n}f_{t,{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}\Bigm|\lambda}\right]=0
by the Law of Total Variance, and the second equality since the summands are iid conditional on
\lambda
.
Since
\lambda_{1}-1
is bounded,
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\left(\lambda_{1}-1\right)^{2}}\right]\to 0
if
\lambda_{1}\xrightarrow{P_{n}}1
, which follows from

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{n}M\right)^{-1}M_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{n}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{I}\left(1\in s_{m,1}\right)\xrightarrow{P_{n}}1

by a law of large numbers, since
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\mathbb{I}\left(1\in s_{m,1}\right)}\right]=P_{n}(1\in s_{m,1})=\pi_{n}
and splits are independent.
Finally, the Gaussian limit follows from Lindeberg’s CLT and the Cramér-Wold device.
∎

Appendix F
Inference with Fast Converging Moments

Consider the setting of
Section

4.1
.
The normal approximation CI
eq.

4.3
may not cover
h(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})
with nominal probability when the variance of any moment function evaluated at the limit parameter
\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
is
0
, that is,

\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}},j}(W)\right]=0

(F.1)

for any
j\in\left[1,\dots,d\right]
.
If that happens, either
\sigma^{2}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}=0
,
\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
is not invertible, or both.
If
\sigma^{2}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}=0
,
eq.

4.1
implies that the centered estimator multiplied by
\sqrt{n}
converges in probability to zero, and the normal approximation in
eq.

4.3
may not be accurate.
Similarly, if
\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
is not invertible,
V^{*}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
is not well-defined, and the normal approximation may be inaccurate.
In this subsection, I provide an approach to inference on
\theta_{\hat{\eta}}
that is general in considering the class of Z-estimators in
Section

3
.

I explore the fact that
eq.

F.1
implies that the empirical moment equation evaluated at
\theta_{\hat{\eta}}
converges faster than the typical
\sqrt{n}
rate to construct a confidence interval for
\theta_{\hat{\eta}}
that is uniformly asymptotically valid regardless of whether
eq.

F.1
happens or not.
The issue discussed in this section is not important for every application.
First, I discuss examples of when one may or may not comfortably assume that
eq.

F.1
does not hold.
Then, I propose a confidence interval, prove its uniform asymptotic validity, and characterize its power properties.
I focus on the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}=\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}^{(2)}
from
Section

3
, and the results can be extended to
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}^{(1)}
and
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}^{(3)}
using similar techniques.

F.1
Examples

In many cases, the researcher can safely assume that
eq.

F.1
does not happen, depending on the setup and definition of
\psi_{\theta,\eta}
.
In other cases, as in Section
7
,
eq.

F.1
can happen under one of the main hypotheses of interest.
I present examples of both cases below.

Example
2

(Revisited)
.

In
2
,
W=(Y,X)
,
Y
is binary, and
{\hat{\eta}}:\mathcal{X}\to\left\{0,1\right\}
is a predictor of
Y
using covariates
X
.
The parameter of interest is a split-sample Z-estimand with
\psi_{\theta,\eta}(y,x)=\mathbb{I}\left(y=\eta(x)\right)-\theta
:

\theta_{\hat{\eta}}=\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\int\mathbb{I}\left(y={\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(x)\right)dP(y,x).

The variance

\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(Y,X)\right]=P\left(Y={\eta^{*}_{P}}(X)\right)\left[1-P\left(Y={\eta^{*}_{P}}(X)\right)\right]

is positive unless
{\eta^{*}_{P}}(X)
always predicts
Y
correctly or always incorrectly.
In practice, predictive algorithms rarely have a near perfect (or imperfect) performance, and in many cases the researcher can confidently assume
\operatorname{Var}_{P}[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(Y,X)]>0
.

∎

Example 9
.

Consider a dataset with covariates
X
, a mean zero continuous outcome
Y\in\mathbb{R}
, and the goal of assessing whether a predictor
{\hat{\eta}}(X)
has predictive power for
Y
.
One way of assessing predictive power for
Y
is by conducting inference on the covariance

\theta_{\hat{\eta}}=\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\int y{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}(x)dP(y,x).

\theta_{\hat{\eta}}
is a Z-estimand with moment function
\psi_{\theta,\eta}(y,x)=y\eta(x)-\theta
, and its limit variance is

\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(Y,X)\right]=\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[Y{\eta^{*}_{P}}(X)\right].

Let
\eta^{*}(x)=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{Y|X=x}\right]
be the limit of
{\hat{\eta}}(x)
.
If
X
has no predictive power for
Y
, for example because
Y
and
X
are independent,
\eta^{*}(x)=0
, and
\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[Y{\eta^{*}_{P}}(X)\right]=0
.
Hence, the CI in
eq.

4.3
may fail to achieve nominal coverage asymptotically.

∎

Remark F.1
.

When
\operatorname{Var}_{P}[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(Y,X)]=0
, the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}
may depend on the specific structure of how
{\hat{\eta}}
is calculated.
Let
Y
be a mean zero scalar random variable,
K=2
,
M=1
, and
(\mathsf{s},\tilde{\mathsf{s}})
be a 2-fold cross-split of the data of equal sizes.
Let
\psi_{\theta,{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(y)=y\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}^{d}-\theta
for some odd positive
d
, where
\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}=\frac{1}{|\tilde{\mathsf{s}}|}\sum_{i\in\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}Y_{i}
.
Then,
\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}}\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}^{d}+\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}}^{d}\right)
and
n^{1/2+d/2}\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}=\frac{1}{2}\left((\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}})(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})^{d}+(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}})^{d}\right)
, which follows a non-trivial distribution that depends on
d
.
If, for example,
d=3
,
(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}})^{d}
is approximately distributed as the cube of a standard normal distribution, and
d=5
leads to a different distribution.
Moreover, the dependence between
(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}})(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})^{d}
and
(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})(\sqrt{n}\bar{y}_{\mathsf{s}})^{d}
is not trivial.

∎

F.2
An Adaptive Confidence Interval

I show how to construct a confidence interval
\hat{C}_{1-\alpha}
that satisfies

\lim_{n\to\infty}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\in\hat{C}_{1-\alpha})=1-\alpha,

regardless of whether
eq.

F.1
may hold or not, by introducing a tuning parameter.
In
Sections

4.2
and
B.5
, I propose a different approach for the particular cases of inference on comparisons between models and in the Generic ML context of
15
, which explicitly account for the dependence across splits.

I construct
\hat{C}_{1-\alpha}
by inverting the test

\begin{cases}H_{0,{\hat{\eta}}}:&h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})=\tau\\
H_{A,{\hat{\eta}}}:&h(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}})\neq\tau,\end{cases}

(F.2)

that is,
\hat{C}_{1-\alpha}
contains all values of
\tau
for which the null hypothesis is not rejected at significance level
\alpha
.
My approach consists of a data-driven procedure to choose one of two p-values for testing
eq.

F.2
:
p_{c}(\tau)
or
p_{e}(\tau)
.

p_{c}(\tau)
is a
conservative
p-value, meant to be valid when
eq.

F.1
holds, that is, the p-value a researcher would use if they knew
eq.

F.1
were true.

p_{e}(\tau)
is an
exact
p-value, coming from the normal approximation
eq.

4.3
, as it achieves exact nominal coverage in large samples when
eq.

F.1
does not hold.
Hence, I test
eq.

F.2
with the p-value
p_{e}(\tau)
when the data suggest that the empirical moment equations are away from zero, and with
p_{c}(\tau)
otherwise.
The idea of using different tests based on pre-testing some condition (in this case, whether the empirical moment equations are away from zero), is similar to
48
, in the context of moment inequalities.
Specifically,

p_{e}(\tau)=2\Phi\left(-\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}\left(h(\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}})-\tau\right)}{\hat{\sigma}_{{\hat{\eta}}}}\right|\right),

\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\tau)=\min_{j\in\left[d\right]}\left|\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},j}(\tau)\right|,

\hat{\Psi}(\tau)=\left\lVert\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\tau)\right\rVert,

a_{n}(\tau)=\mathbb{I}\left(\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\tau)\hat{\Psi}(\tau)>\gamma_{n}\right),

p(\tau)=a_{n}(\tau)p_{e}(\tau)+\left[1-a_{n}(\tau)\right]p_{c}(\tau),

\hat{C}_{1-\alpha}=\left\{\tau\in\mathbb{R}:p(\tau)\leq\alpha\right\}.

The idea behind
a_{n}
is that
\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\tau)\xrightarrow{P}0
when
\operatorname{Var}_{P}[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W)]=0
.
The sequence
\gamma_{n}
is a tuning parameter that should ideally be specified before the data analysis.
The properties of
\gamma_{n}
and
p_{c}(\tau)
are specified in
F.1
below.

Assumption F.1
.

The following conditions hold:

(i)

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P(p_{c}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\leq\alpha)\leq\alpha
;

(ii)

n\gamma_{n}\to\gamma\in(0,\infty)
;

(iii)

The set
\mathcal{P}
can be decomposed as
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{+}\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{0}
, where

(a)

For every
\varepsilon>0
,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{+}}\sup_{\left\lVert\theta-\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right\rVert>\varepsilon}-\left\lVert\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta)\right\rVert<0=\left\lVert\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})\right\rVert,

\Psi_{\eta}
is differentiable at
\theta_{\eta}
for
\eta\in H
, and for some
\bar{c}_{1}>0
,

\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{+}}\left|\det\left(\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\right)\right|\geq\bar{c}_{1};

(b)

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\left\lVert\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}})\right\rVert=0
,
\theta_{\hat{\eta}}\xrightarrow{P}\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
for some
\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}\in\Theta^{\prime}
uniformly in
P\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, and

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\min_{j\in\left[d\right]}\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\eta^{*}_{P}},j}(W)\right]=0.

∎

F.1(i)
requires the p-value
p_{c}(\tau)
to be valid, even if conservative, including when
\operatorname{Var}_{P}[\psi_{\theta_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}},{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(W)]=0
.
Constructing
p_{c}(\tau)
is context-specific, but a conservative, trivially valid option is
p_{c}(\tau)=1
.
Note that this option does not lead to an unbounded CI since
a_{n}(\tau)=1
with probability approaching one for values of
\tau
far from
\theta_{\hat{\eta}}
.

F.1(ii)
requires
\gamma_{n}
to converge to zero at the
n^{-1}
rate.

F.1(iii)
substitutes and weakens
B.1(iii)
and
B.1(v)
.
It allows
\dot{\Psi}_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}
to be singular and
\lVert\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P}}}(\theta)\rVert=0

to have multiple solutions for
\theta
when the variance of
\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\eta^{*}_{P}},j}(W)
is zero for some
j
. Valid inference is achieved in these cases since
a_{n}=0
with probability approaching one.
Note that
B.1(iii)
and
B.1(v)
imply
F.1(iii)
since
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{+}
, and if

\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\min_{j\in\left[d\right]}\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}},{\eta^{*}_{P}},j}(W)\right]>0,

F.1(iii)
implies both
B.1(iii)
and
B.1(v)
.
I establish the uniform asymptotic validity of
\hat{C}_{1-\alpha}
, and explore its power properties.

Theorem F.1
.

(Uniform Asymptotic Validity of
\hat{C}_{1-\alpha}
)

Let Assumptions
B.1(i)
-
B.1(iv)
and
F.1
hold.
Then,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\in\hat{C}_{1-\alpha})\geq 1-\alpha.

∎

I show that the hypothesis test
eq.

F.2
, where
H_{0,{\hat{\eta}}}
is rejected if
p(\tau)>\alpha
, has power approaching
1
for fixed alternatives and non-trivial power for some sequences of local alternative hypotheses.
I compare my test with an oracle test that correctly picks
p_{e}(\tau)
or
p_{c}(\tau)
depending on the asymptotic behavior of
\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}
.
In order to study local power, I consider sequences
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
under different regimes for the limit behavior of
\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert
and the variance of
\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(W)
.
Let

v_{n}^{2}=\min_{j\in\left[d\right]}\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},j}(W)\right].

The oracle test is defined by

p^{*}(\tau)=\begin{cases}p_{c}(\tau)\text{, if }v_{n}\to 0\text{ and }\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert=O_{P_{n}}(1),\\
p_{e}(\tau)\text{, otherwise}.\end{cases}

This test is infeasible since it depends on the sequence of DGPs
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.
For the different regimes, I compare the limits

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}\left(p(\tau)\leq\alpha\right),

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}\left(p^{*}(\tau)\leq\alpha\right).

Theorem F.2
.

Let
B.1(i)
-
B.1(iv)
and
F.1
hold,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence such that the limits
v
,
\pi_{\alpha}
and
\pi^{*}_{\alpha}
exist.
Assume
p_{c}(\tau)
is an independent Bernoulli random variable taking value
0
with probability
\alpha
and
1
with probability
1-\alpha
(that is, it rejects the null with probability
\alpha
).
Then, the relationships in
Table

5
hold, where each row defines a separate regime for
\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert
.

Table 5
:
Power Comparison by Regime

nv_{n}^{2}=o(1)

nv_{n}^{2}=O(1)^{*}

nv_{n}^{2}\to\infty^{**}

v_{n}^{2}\to v^{2}>0

\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert\xrightarrow{P_{n}}\infty

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}<\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha<\pi_{\alpha}<\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\pi_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}=1

\pi_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}=1

\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert=O_{P_{n}}(1)^{***}

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}<\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}<\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}<\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha<\pi_{\alpha}<\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert\xrightarrow{P_{n}}0

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

\alpha=\pi_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}

* Assumes
nv_{n}^{2}\nrightarrow 0
; ** Assumes
v_{n}^{2}\to 0
; *** Assumes
\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert\neq o_{P_{n}}(1)
.

∎

F.3
Proofs and Extra Definitions

Proof of
F.1
.

Let
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
be such that

v=\lim_{n\to\infty}\min_{j\in\left[d\right]}\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},j}(W)\right]

exists.

If
v>0
,

p(\tau)\geq p_{e}(\tau)
, and

P_{n}(\theta_{{\hat{\eta}}}\in\hat{C}_{1-\alpha})\geq 1-\alpha+o(1)

by
3.1
.

For
v=0
, note that by
E.1
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})-\Psi_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})-\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\right)+o_{P_{n}}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\left(\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}})-\Psi_{{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}})\right)+o_{P_{n}}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}}(\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}})+o_{P_{n}}(1),

and, for any
j\in\left[d\right]
,

\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\frac{\sqrt{n}}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},j}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\right]\leq\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\psi_{\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}},j}(W)\right]+o(1).

If
v=0
,

\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\frac{\sqrt{n}}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},{j_{n}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\right]\to 0

for some
(j_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, and hence

\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\min_{j\in\left[d\right]}\left|\frac{\sqrt{n}}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}},j}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\right|\right]\to 0.

As a consequence,

P_{n}\Bigl(\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})}_{=o_{P_{n}}(1)}\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}(\tau)}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}>\underbrace{n\gamma_{n}}_{=\gamma+o(1)}\Bigr)\to 0,

and
a_{n}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\xrightarrow{P_{n}}0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
F.2
.

Define

\Psi_{n}(\tau)=\left\lVert P_{n}\frac{1}{MK}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in r}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathsf{s},{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}(\tau)\right\rVert.

First, let
\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert\xrightarrow{P_{n}}\infty^{*}
.
If
nv_{n}\to\infty
,

P_{n}\Bigl(\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}\Bigl[\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}(\tau)-\sqrt{n}\Psi_{n}(\tau)}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}+\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\Psi_{n}(\tau)}_{\xrightarrow{P_{n}}\infty}\Bigr]\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}(\tau)}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}>\underbrace{n\gamma_{n}}_{=\gamma+o(1)}\Bigr)\xrightarrow{P_{n}}1,

since

\sqrt{n}\left(\Psi_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(\tau)-\Psi_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})\right)=\left(\dot{\Psi}_{\eta^{*}_{P_{n}}}+o_{P_{n}}(1)\right)\sqrt{n}\left(\tau-\theta_{\hat{\eta}}\right).

If
nv_{n}=O(1)
,

P_{n}\Bigl(\underbrace{v_{n}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}\Bigl[\underbrace{v_{n}\sqrt{n}(\hat{\Psi}(\tau)-\Psi_{n}(\tau))}_{=o_{P_{n}}(1)}+\underbrace{v_{n}\sqrt{n}\Psi_{n}(\tau)}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}\Bigr]\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}(\tau)}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}>\underbrace{n\gamma_{n}}_{=\gamma+o(1)}\Bigr)

is
O_{P_{n}}(1)
, and
a_{n}(\tau)=O_{P_{n}}(1)
.
If
nv_{n}=o(1)
,
v_{n}\sqrt{n}\Psi_{n}(\tau)=o_{P_{n}}(1)
, and
a_{n}\xrightarrow{P_{n}}0
.

Second, let
\sqrt{n}\left\lVert\theta_{\hat{\eta}}-\tau\right\rVert=O_{P_{n}}(1)
, and hence
\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}(\tau)=O_{P_{n}}(1)
.
It follows that

P_{n}\Bigl(\underbrace{v_{n}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}_{\rm min}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}\underbrace{\sqrt{n}\hat{\Psi}(\tau)}_{=O_{P_{n}}(1)}>v_{n}^{-1}\underbrace{n\gamma_{n}}_{=\gamma+o(1)}\Bigr)

is
O_{P_{n}}(1)
if
v_{n}^{2}\to v^{2}>0
, and converges to zero if
v_{n}\to 0
.

Finally, the last row follows since
p_{e}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})-p_{e}(\tau)=o_{P_{n}}(1)
.
Note that
p_{e}(\theta_{\hat{\eta}})-p_{e}(\tau)>o_{P_{n}}(1)
for the second row.
∎

Appendix G
Note on Comparing Two Nonparametric Models

I discuss an extension of the setting of
Section

4.2
for comparing
\theta_{\hat{\eta}}
to the performance of another model
{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}
, computed with the same split-sample approach as
{\hat{\eta}}
.

Let

\mathcal{S}=\left(\mathsf{s}_{m,k}\right)_{m\in\left[M\right],k\in\left[K\right]}

and denote the split-specific models
{\hat{\eta}}=({\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}
and
{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}=({\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}
, where
{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}=\mathcal{A}(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})
and
{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(D_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}})
, that is, the two models are trained using the same sample but different algorithms.
For example,
{\hat{\eta}}
could be estimated with random forests while
{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}
could be estimated with neural networks.
Denote

{\hat{\delta}}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}^{(1)}=\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}-\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}\right)_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}

and

{\hat{\delta}}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}^{(2)}=\left(\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}-\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}\right)_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}.

{\hat{\delta}}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}^{(1)}
can be used for testing whether
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\geq\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}
for all
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}
versus the alternative that
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}<\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}
for at least one
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, similarly to
Section

4.2.1
and
4.2
.
Note that the Donsker and rate conditions in
4.1(ii)
are not required for
4.2
.
They are used only for the pointwise
4.3
to cover the case
\theta_{\eta^{*}_{P}}=\theta_{b_{P}}
.
Similarly,
{\hat{\delta}}_{{\hat{\eta}},{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}}^{(2)}
can be used to test whether
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}\geq\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}
for all
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}
versus the alternative that
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}^{\prime}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}}<\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}
for at least one
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Appendix H
Additional Results

Proposition H.1
.

In the context of
E.1
, let
T=\left\{t\right\}
and
\mathcal{P}=\left\{P\right\}
be singletons, and let
D.1
hold for
P
and
f_{\eta,t}
measurable with
f_{\eta,t}(w)<\infty
for all
w
.
Then,
E.1
holds.

∎

Proof of
H.1
.

E.1(i)
and
E.1(ii)
hold trivially.

E.1(iii)
holds by taking
f_{\eta,t}
as its own envelope.
The uniform integrability condition
E.1(iv)
is implied by the
2+\delta
assumption
D.1(i)
.

E.1(v)
holds trivially.

E.1(vi)
holds since both covering and bracketing numbers are equal to
1
with singleton
T
.
Finally,
E.2
follows since

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P}}\left[{\operatorname{Var}_{P}\left[f_{{\hat{\eta}}_{\tilde{\xi}}}(W)-f_{\eta^{*}}(W)\Bigm|D_{\tilde{\xi}}\right]}\right]\to 0,

as established under
D.1(ii)
in the proof of
D.1

eq.

D.13
, since convergence in
L_{1}
implies convergence in probability.
∎

Lemma H.1
.

In the context of
E.1
,

Z_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(M_{i}=c\right)\xrightarrow{P_{n}}\dbinom{M}{c}\pi^{c}(1-\pi)^{M-c}.

Proof.

I show that
\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{Z_{n}}\right]=\dbinom{M}{c}{\pi_{n}}^{c}(1-{\pi_{n}})^{M-c}
and
\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[Z_{n}\right]\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
By definition,
M_{i}=\left|\left\{s\in\left\{s_{m,1}\right\}_{m\in\left[M\right]}:i\in s\right\}\right|
.

\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{Z_{n}}\right]=\,{\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\mathbb{I}\left(M_{1}=c\right)}\right]=P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\right)
since all
M_{i}
are equally distributed for any
i
.
The event
\left\{M_{i}=c\right\}
is equivalent to the event that observation
i
is chosen in exactly
c
of the
M
splits of the sample.
Since the splits are independent,
M_{i}
follows a binomial distribution with parameters
M
and
\pi_{n}
.
Hence, the probability of this event is
\dbinom{M}{c}{\pi_{n}}^{c}(1-{\pi_{n}})^{M-c}
.

To show that
\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[Z_{n}\right]\to 0
, I use the fact that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[Z_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=n^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\mathbb{I}\left(M_{i}=c\right)\right]+n^{-2}\sum_{i\neq j}\,{\operatorname{Cov}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\mathbb{I}\left(M_{i}=c\right),\mathbb{I}\left(M_{j}=c\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\operatorname{Var}_{P_{n}}\left[\mathbb{I}\left(M_{1}=c\right)\right]+n^{-2}n(n-1)\,{\operatorname{Cov}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\mathbb{I}\left(M_{1}=c\right),\mathbb{I}\left(M_{2}=c\right)}\right].

Hence, it’s enough to show that

\,{\operatorname{Cov}_{P_{n}}}\left[{\mathbb{I}\left(M_{1}=c\right),\mathbb{I}\left(M_{2}=c\right)}\right]=P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c,M_{2}=c\right)-P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\right)^{2}\to 0.

I show that
P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\Bigm|M_{2}=c\right)\to P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\right)
.
Note
b=\pi_{n}n
is the number of draws in each split.
Using combinatorial arguments, the conditional probability is given by

\displaystyle P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\Bigm|M_{2}=c\right)=\sum_{t=0}^{c}

\displaystyle\dbinom{c}{t}\dbinom{M-c}{c-t}\left(\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-2}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b-1}}\right)^{t}\left(1-\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-2}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b-1}}\right)^{c-t}

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-1}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b}}\right)^{c-t}\left(1-\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-1}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b}}\right)^{M-2c+t}.

t
represents the number of splits that contain both observations 1 and 2.
Since observation 2 is chosen in
c
splits,
0\leq t\leq c
.
There are
\dbinom{c}{t}
ways of choosing among the
c
splits that contain observation 2, which
t
will also contain observation 1.
There are
\dbinom{M-c}{c-t}
ways of choosing the remaining
c-t
splits that contain observation 1 but not 2.

\left(\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-2}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b-1}}\right)^{t}
is the probability of choosing observation 1 in the
t
splits that contain both observations.

\left(1-\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-2}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b-1}}\right)^{c-t}
is the probability of not choosing observation 1 in the remaining
c-t
splits that contain observation 2.

\left(\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-1}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b}}\right)^{c-t}
is the probability of choosing observation 1 in the
c-t
splits that contain observation 1 but not 2.
Finally,
\left(1-\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-1}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b}}\right)^{M-2c+t}
is the probability of not choosing observation 1 in the remaining
M-2c+t
splits that contain neither observation.

For large
n
, we can approximate the combinatorial terms:

\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-2}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b-1}}=\frac{(n-2)!}{(b-2)!(n-b)!}\left(\frac{(n-1)!}{(b-1)!(n-b)!}\right)^{-1}=\frac{b-1}{n-1}={\pi_{n}}+o(1).

Similarly,

\frac{\dbinom{n-2}{b-1}}{\dbinom{n-1}{b}}=\frac{(n-2)!}{(b-1)!(n-b-1)!}\left(\frac{(n-1)!}{b!(n-b-1)!}\right)^{-1}=\frac{b}{n-1}={\pi_{n}}+o(1).

It follows that

\displaystyle P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\Bigm|M_{2}=c\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{c}\dbinom{c}{t}\dbinom{M-c}{c-t}{\pi_{n}}^{t}(1-{\pi_{n}})^{c-t}{\pi_{n}}^{c-t}(1-{\pi_{n}})^{M-2c+t}+o(1)

\displaystyle={\pi_{n}}^{c}(1-{\pi_{n}})^{M-c}\sum_{t=0}^{c}\dbinom{c}{t}\dbinom{M-c}{c-t}+o(1)

\displaystyle={\pi_{n}}^{c}(1-{\pi_{n}})^{M-c}\dbinom{M}{c}+o(1)

\displaystyle=P_{n}\left(M_{1}=c\right)+o(1),

where the third equality uses Vandermonde’s Identity.
∎

Appendix I
Covariate Adjustment in Randomized Trials

Let
W=(Y,A,X)
, where
Y\in\mathbb{R}
is an observed outcome,
A
is a binary (randomized) treatment assignment indicator, and
X\in\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a set of covariates, for some
d\geq 1
.
Let
Y(1),Y(0)
denote potential outcomes respectively under treatment and control, and
Y=AY(1)+(1-A)Y(0)
.
In the simplest form of an RCT,
A\perp(X,Y(1),Y(0))
.
In this setting, the ATE
\theta
can be identified from the regression

Y=\alpha+\theta A+\varepsilon.

(I.1)

The covariates are not necessary for identification of
\theta
.
However, adding regressors in (
I.1
) can lead to power improvement by reducing the variance of the error term
\varepsilon
and thus the asymptotic variance of the least squares estimator of
\theta
.
One approach to incorporating covariates is through a covariate-adjustment term
\eta(X)
:

Y=\alpha_{\eta}+\theta_{\eta}D+\beta_{\eta}\eta(X)+\varepsilon.

(I.2)

If
A\perp(X,Y(1),Y(0))
,
\theta_{\eta}=\theta
does not depend on
\eta
. Still, its OLS estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\eta}
does depend on
\eta
.
In practice, one needs to estimate
\eta
with a model
{\hat{\eta}}
.
Inference becomes challenging if the same data is used to estimate both
{\hat{\eta}}
and
\hat{\theta}_{{\hat{\eta}}}
because the observations in
eq.

I.2
become no longer iid.
The asymptotic distribution of
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{\hat{\eta}}-\theta_{\hat{\eta}})
can be characterized following
Section

3
, specifically
3.1
.
